Dear Mr Monks 
Acknowledgment of request for internal review by Ms Vida Reid 
Section 100(2) of the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 
I refer to the email received by the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission on 25 January 
2023 requesting an internal review of the decision dated 20 December 2022 to impose a 
banning order against your client, Ms Vida Reid 
Internal review process 
A delegate of the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commissioner who was not involved in the making of 
the original decision will conduct the internal review. The delegate will consider the merits of the 
original decision and must then decide to do one of the following: 
•
confirm the decision; or
•
vary the decision; or
•
set the decision aside and substitute a new decision.
Further submissions 
In the email dated 25 January 2023, you did not explain why your client is seeking an internal 
review or provide us with information in support of the request for review. 
If your client wishes to make any submissions in support of her request for an internal review, or to 
provide us with any information to consider in our internal review, please provide it by email to 
Review@ndiscommission.gov.au by 5pm on 20 February 2023. If no submissions are received by this 
date, we will proceed to make a decision on the internal review. 
If your client chooses to make submissions, she should be aware that giving false and misleading 
information to the NDIS Commission is a serious offence. 
Due date for internal review decision 
We are required to make a decision on the internal review within 90 days from the day we received 
the request for review. This means our decision is due to be made by 24 April 2023.
1295
T60


===== PAGE 126 =====

Effect of internal review 
In accordance with section 100 of the NDIS Act, a request for internal review does not affect the 
operation of the original decision or prevent the taking of action to give effect to the original decision. 
This means that the decision to impose a banning order will continue to apply until we make our 
internal review decision. 
Withdrawal 
You can choose to withdraw this request for internal review at any time before we make our 
decision on the review. You can withdraw the request by email to

PRIVACY NOTICE 
The NDIS Commission is bound by the Australian Privacy Principles outlined in the Privacy Act 1988 
(Cth) (Privacy Act), which regulate how entities may collect, use, disclose and store personal 
information. 
The NDIS Commission is also bound by the information management provisions of the NDIS Act. 
These provide that if the NDIS Commission holds information about a person in its records then that 
information is regarded as ‘protected Commission information’ under the National Disability 
Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (NDIS Act). Unauthorised use and disclosure of protected Commission 
information by any person is a criminal offence under the NDIS Act. 
Collection of personal information 
The NDIS Commission may collect personal information from you to process and decide your request 
for internal review. 
If you do not provide us with the personal information sought by us in relation to your review, the 
NDIS Commission may not be able to process your request for an internal review. 
Use and disclosure of personal/protected information 
Personal information obtained from you in relation to your review will be used and disclosed for the 
purposes of processing and deciding your request for internal review. 
We will only use or disclose your personal information for another purpose or in any other 
circumstances if: 
•
you consent, or
•
it is required or authorised by law, including under the Privacy Act or NDIS Act.
The NDIS Act authorises the use and disclosure of protected Commission information (including 
personal information) in certain circumstances, which may include: 
•
disclosing information to the National Disability Insurance Agency (NDIA), including for the
purposes of notifying the NDIA of matters relating to the registration of an NDIS provider
•
using or disclosing information for the purposes of performing the NDIS Commissioner’s
functions, such as carrying out its compliance and enforcement functions
•
disclosing information to other relevant parties, including other Commonwealth, state or
territory agencies, regulatory bodies or professional associations
•
disclosing personal information to an overseas recipient to support certain NDIS Commission
functions.
Personal information obtained through the internal review process will be stored and held in 
accordance with the NDIS Commission’s obligations under the Privacy Act and the Archives Act 1983 
(Cth). 
The NDIS Commission’s privacy policy 
The NDIS Commission’s privacy policy (available at www.ndiscommission.gov.au/privacy) sets out 
how the NDIS Commission complies with the Privacy Act. The policy describes: 
•
the kinds of personal information we collect and hold
•
the purposes for which we collect, hold, use and disclose personal information
1297
T60

•
how you can seek access to and correct personal information
•
how we deal with any unauthorised disclosures of personal information
•
how to make a privacy complaint.

NOTICE OF REQUIREMENT TO GIVE INFORMATION BY APPEARING BEFORE A COMMISSION OFFICER 
SECTION 56 OF THE NATIONAL DISABILITY INSURANCE SCHEME ACT 2013 (NDIS Act) 
 
Pursuant to section 55A(1) of the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (NDIS Act), l, Philip 
Halton, delegate of the Commissioner of the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission (the 
Commissioner), require you to appear in person before Matt Read, a Commission officer, to answer 
questions. 
 
You are required to attend at  10:00 am at the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission office at Level 1, 
200 St Georges Terrace, Perth WA 6000 on Monday 13 February 2023.  
 
This notice is issued to you under section 56 of the NDIS Act. 
 
Basis for this notice 
I give this notice in accordance with section 56 of the NDIS Act. I require you to give information that I 
reasonably believe you can give, that may be relevant to one or more of the matters listed in section 55A(2)  
of the NDIS Act. In respect of this notice, the matters are: 
 
 
whether Ms Vida Reid (Ms Reid), a person subject of a banning order made under section 73ZN of 
the NDIS Act, is providing or has provided supports or services in contravention of the order 
(section 55A(2)(e)); 
 
whether Mr Simon Reid (Mr Reid), a person subject of a banning order made under section 73ZN 
of the NDIS Act, is providing or has provided supports or services in contravention of the order 
(section 55A(2)(e)); 
 
whether Dream Big Employment Pty Ltd (Dream Big), as an NDIS provider, is complying or has 
complied with the requirements of the NDIS Code of Conduct, particularly sections 6(a)1 and 6(d) 
2, in the provision of supports and services to Ms Annette Ogden (Ms Ogden) and other NDIS 
participants, as required by section 73V of the NDIS Act (section 55A(2)(d)); 
 
whether Ms Tori Fitton (Ms Fitton), as a person employed by a NDIS provider (Dream Big), 
complied with the requirements of the NDIS Code of Conduct, particularly sections 6(a) and 6(d), 
in the provision of supports and services to Ms Ogden, as required by section 73V of the NDIS Act 
(section 55A(2)(d)); and 
 
the functions of the Commissioner with respect to monitoring, compliance and enforcement of 
Dream Big’s, Ms Reid’s, Mr Reid’s and Ms Fitton’s obligations under the NDIS Act (section 
55A(2)(f)).

during the questioning, and if you will be, the name of the legal representative or other person. 
 
Please ensure that you bring current photographic identification to the interview.  
 
Non-compliance with this notice 
Refusal or failure, without reasonable excuse, to comply with this notice is an offence under section 57(1) 
of the NDIS Act punishable on conviction by a penalty not exceeding $8,250 for an individual.  
 
Under section 57(3) of the NDIS Act, it is a reasonable excuse for an individual to refuse to give 
information on the ground that to do so might tend to incriminate the individual or expose the individual 
to a penalty. This provision can excuse you from giving information in response to specific questions 
where the information might tend to incriminate you or expose you to a penalty. It does not excuse you 
from appearing before a Commission officer.  
 
It is also a serious offence to provide false or misleading information or produce false or misleading 
documents to the Commission (see sections 137.1(1) of The Criminal Code which is in a schedule to the 
Criminal Code Act 1995 (Cth)).

Schedule 1 
 
Section 55A Power of Commissioner to obtain information from other persons to ensure the integrity of 
the National Disability Insurance Scheme etc. 
(1) If the Commissioner reasonably believes that a person, other than a prospective participant or a 
person receiving supports or services from an NDIS provider, has information, or has custody or 
control of a document, that may be relevant to one or more of the matters mentioned in 
subsection (2), the Commissioner may require the person to give the information, or produce the 
document, to the Commissioner. 
(2) The matters are as follows: 
(a) whether an NDIS provider is contravening subsection 73B(2) (requirement to be a 
registered NDIS provider); 
(b) whether a person applying for registration under subsection 73E(1) satisfies the 
requirements mentioned in that subsection; 
(c) whether a registered NDIS provider is meeting the conditions of registration mentioned 
in subsection 73F(1); 
(d) whether an NDIS provider, or a person employed or otherwise engaged by an NDIS 
provider, is complying with the requirements of the NDIS Code of Conduct; 
(e) if an NDIS provider, or a person employed or otherwise engaged by an NDIS provider, is 
subject to a banning order—whether the person is providing supports or services in 
contravention of the order; 
(f) the functions of the Commissioner. 
 
Section 56 Written notice of requirement 
(1) A requirement under section 55 or 55A must be made by written notice given to the person of 
whom the requirement is made. 
(2) The notice must specify: 
(a) the nature of the information or document that is required to be given or produced; and 
(b) how the person is to give the information or produce the document; and 
(c) the period within which the person is to give the information or produce the document 
to the Agency or Commissioner; and 
(d) if the notice is given by the CEO—the Agency officer to whom the information is to be 
given or the document is to be produced; and 
(da) if the notice is given by the Commissioner—the Commission officer to whom the 
information is to be given or the document is to be produced 
(e) that the notice is given under this section. 
(3) The period specified under paragraph (2)(c) must be at least 14 days beginning on the day on 
which the notice is given. 
(4) If the notice is given by the CEO: 
(a) the notice may require the person to give the information by appearing before a 
specified Agency officer to answer questions; and 
1304
T61.1


===== PAGE 135 =====

 
 
6 
(b) if paragraph (a) applies—the notice must specify a time and place at which the person is 
to appear, which must be at least 14 days after the notice is given. 
(5) If the notice is given by the Commissioner: 
(a) the notice may require the person to give the information by appearing before a 
specified Commission officer to answer questions; and 
(b) if paragraph (a) applies—the notice must specify a time and place at which the person is 
to appear, which must be at least 14 days after the notice is given. 
 
Section 57 Offence - refusal or failure to comply with requirement 
(1) A person must not refuse or fail to comply with a requirement under section 55 or 55A to give 
information or produce a document. 
Penalty:   
 
30 penalty units. 
Note:  If a body corporate is convicted of an offence against this subsection, subsection 4B(3) of 
the Crimes Act 1914 allows a court to impose a fine of up to 5 times the penalty stated 
above. 
(2) Subsection (1) does not apply if the person has a reasonable excuse. 
Note: A defendant bears an evidential burden in relation to the matter in this subsection: see 
subsection 13.3(3) of the Criminal Code. 
(3) It is a reasonable excuse for an individual to refuse or fail to give information or produce a 
document on the ground that to do so might tend to incriminate the individual or expose the 
individual to a penalty. 
Division 137 – Criminal Code  
Criminal Code Act 1995 
137.1  False or misleading information 
             (1)  A person commits an offence if: 
                     (a)  the person gives information to another person; and 
                     (b)  the person does so knowing that the information: 
                              (i)  is false or misleading; or 
                             (ii)  omits any matter or thing without which the information is misleading; and 
                     (c)  any of the following subparagraphs applies: 
                              (i)  the information is given to a Commonwealth entity; 
                             (ii)  the information is given to a person who is exercising powers or performing functions 
under, or in connection with, a law of the Commonwealth; 
                            (iii)  the information is given in compliance or purported compliance with a law of the 
Commonwealth. 
Penalty:  Imprisonment for 12 months. 
             (1A)  Absolute liability applies to each of the subparagraph (1)(c)(i), (ii) and (iii) elements of the 
offence. 
1305
T61.1


===== PAGE 136 =====

7 
(2) Subsection (1) does not apply as a result of subparagraph (1)(b)(i) if the information is not false
or misleading in a material particular.
Note:  
 A defendant bears an evidential burden in relation to the matter in subsection (2). 
See subsection 13.3(3). 
(3) Subsection (1) does not apply as a result of subparagraph (1)(b)(ii) if the information did not
omit any matter or thing without which the information is misleading in a material particular.
Note:  
 A defendant bears an evidential burden in relation to the matter in subsection (3). 
See subsection 13.3(3). 
(4) Subsection (1) does not apply as a result of subparagraph (1)(c)(i) if, before the information
was given by a person to the Commonwealth entity, the Commonwealth entity did not take
reasonable steps to inform the person of the existence of the offence against subsection (1). 
Note:  
 A defendant bears an evidential burden in relation to the matter in subsection (4). 
See subsection 13.3(3). 
(5) Subsection (1) does not apply as a result of subparagraph (1)(c)(ii) if, before the information
was given by a person (the first person) to the person mentioned in that
subparagraph (the second person), the second person did not take reasonable steps to 
inform the first person of the existence of the offence against subsection (1). 
Note:  
 A defendant bears an evidential burden in relation to the matter in subsection (5). 
See subsection 13.3(3). 
(6) For the purposes of subsections (4) and (5), it is sufficient if the following form of words is
used:
“Giving false or misleading information is a serious offence”.

providers or providing support, funding, guidance or assistance of any form to an NDIS provider.

Dear Mr Monks 
Intention to take into account new information received by the NDIS Quality and Safeguards 
Commission 
Internal review of banning order – Simon Reid 
I refer to the email received by the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission (the NDIS Commission) 
on 25 January 2023 requesting an internal review of the decision made on 21 December 2022 to 
impose a banning order against your client, Mr Simon Reid. 
I am the delegate that will be making the decision on the internal review. 
I am due to make the internal review decision by 24 April 2023. 
Intention to take into account new information and vary banning order 
In considering the request for internal review, I have become aware of information not considered 
by the original delegate relating to Mr Reid’s alleged involvement in businesses with links to the 
NDIS, such as Dream Big Employment Pty Ltd and Vitality WA Pty Ltd. I have set out details of the 
new information in Attachment A to this letter.  
The banning order currently prohibits Mr Reid from providing disability supports and services to 
NDIS funded participants for a period of five years (effective from 21 December 2022).  
Taking into account the information at Attachment A, I intend on varying the banning order against 
Mr Reid to make it explicit that he is to have no involvement with or in the NDIS sector, in any 
capacity. This includes being prohibited from providing labour hire workers to support NDIS 
providers or providing support, funding, guidance or assistance of any form to an NDIS provider.  
I am writing now to give your client the opportunity to make any submissions to me in relation to 
both this information and my proposed variation to the terms of the banning order. 
1346
T65


===== PAGE 177 =====

NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission 
2 
If your client wishes to make submissions, please provide them by email to 
review@ndiscommission.gov.au  by 5pm on 5 April 2023. If no submissions are received by this 
date, I will proceed to make the internal review decision. 
If your client wishes to request additional time to provide submissions, please submit such a request 
via email to review@ndiscommission.gov.au by 31 March 2023.   
If your client chooses to make submissions, he should be aware that giving false and misleading 
information to the NDIS Commission is a serious offence. 
Contact details 
Should you have any queries about this matter, you can contact Ms Pascal Moussa by telephone to 
02 9942 5726 or by email to pascal.moussa@ndiscommission.gov.au  
Yours sincerely 
Sian Leathem

At Vitality Employment , we are dedicated to providing exceptional support services 
to disability providers, helping their clients to lead fulfilling and independent lives. 
We are currently expanding our team and are seeking passionate and experienced 
individuals to join us in this mission.
As a Disability Support Worker, you will have the opportunity to make a real 
difference in the lives of those you support. You will be responsible for providing 
day-to-day support to people with disabilities, helping them with activities of daily 
living, social and recreational activities, and any other tasks required to enhance their 
quality of life.
Key responsibilities include:

Providing personal care and support to individuals with disabilities

Assisting with daily living activities, such as meal preparation and
housekeeping

Supporting individuals in accessing community services and participating in
recreational activities

Building positive relationships with individuals, their families and other
stakeholders

qualifications:


Notice of internal review decision 
Section 100 of the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (NDIS Act) 
Dear Mr Monks 
I refer to the email received by the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission (the NDIS Commission) 
on 21 February 2023 requesting an internal review of the decision made on 20 December 2022 to 
impose a banning order against your client, Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd (FFG), for a period of 10 
years under section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act (the original decision).  
I am the delegate of the NDIS Commissioner for the purposes of section 100 of the NDIS Act, and am 
authorised to make a decision on this internal review.  
The internal review decision 
I have decided to vary the original decision. This means the banning order made against FFG 
remains in place, however it is changed or altered in some respect.  
The variation I have decided to impose is to reduce the duration of the banning order from 10 years 
to 3 years. In my view, this corresponds with the amount of time it is reasonably expected to take 
for criminal proceedings relating to FFG to be finally determined. Upon determination of those 
proceedings, the banning order against FFG can be revisited and consideration given to whether it 
should be varied any further (or revoked entirely).  
I enclose a copy of the banning order as varied, and the reasons for my review decision at 
Attachments A and B to this notice. 
Consequences of review decision 
The original banning order made against FFG is not disturbed and continues to have effect from 
5:30pm on 20 December 2022 (AWST), however it will only remain in force until 12:00am on 21 
December 2025 (AWST).  
1375
T71.1


===== PAGE 206 =====

Engaging in conduct that breaches a banning order is a contravention of section 73ZN(10) of the 
NDIS Act. A contravention of a banning order may attract a civil penalty of up to 1,000 penalty units, 
however for a corporation this may be up to 5 times the amount of the maximum penalty, 
amounting to $1,375,000. 
External review rights 
If FFG is dissatisfied with my review decision, it may apply to the Administrative Appeals Tribunal 
(AAT) for a review of the decision. I note this internal review decision was made outside of the 
statutory timeframe imposed by section 100 (6A) of the NDIS Act. However, FFG is still able to apply 
within 28 days after receiving this notice of decision to the AAT for review of this decision pursuant 
to section 29(3)(b)(i) of the Administrative Appeals Tribunal Act 1975 (Cth). Further information 
about applying to the AAT for a review can be found at https://www.aat.gov.au/apply-for-a-
review/national-disability-insurance-scheme-ndis or by contacting the AAT on 1800 228 333. 
Variation or revocation of banning order 
Section 73ZO of the NDIS Act allows the NDIS Commissioner to vary or revoke a banning order at 
any time, on her own initiative, or in response to an application by the person against whom the 
order was made. If FFG is minded to request a variation or revocation of the banning order against 
it, an application must be in accordance with the matters set out in section 73ZO of the NDIS Act. 
Contact details  
Should you have any queries about this matter, please do not hesitate to contact Pascal Moussa, 
Lawyer, on 02 9942 5726 or at pascal.moussa@ndiscommission.gov.au.  
Yours sincerely 
Sian Leathem  
Complaints Commissioner  
NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission 
8 June 2023 
Encl: Attachment A – Banning order (varied on 8 June 2023) issued to FFG 
    Attachment B – Statement of Reasons for Internal Review Decision 
1376
T71.1


===== PAGE 207 =====

NATIONAL DISABILITY INSURANCE SCHEME ACT 2013 (CTH) 
SECTION 73ZN BANNING ORDER 
I, Sian Leathem, as delegate of the Commissioner of the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission 
under the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (the NDIS Act), make this banning order 
with respect to Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd.    
I make the banning order under section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act on the basis that the registration 
of Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd as a registered NDIS provider was revoked on 20 December 2022.  
Pursuant to section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act, I prohibit Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd from 
providing supports or services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded participants for a period of 
three (3) years from the date that this order takes effect.  
My reasons for making this order are set out in the enclosed statement of reasons.  
This order varies the banning order made on 20 December 2022. 
This order takes effect from 20 December 2022 until 12:00am on 21 December 2025 (AWST). 
Sian Leathem  
Complaints Commissioner  
NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission 
8 June 2023 
1377
T71.1


===== PAGE 208 =====

NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission 
Attachment B 
Statement of Reasons 
Internal review decision – section 100 of the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (NDIS 
Act) 
Preliminary matters 
1.
This statement of reasons outlines why I have decided to vary the banning order made on 20
December 2022 under section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act, which prohibited Forward Focused
Group Pty Ltd (FFG) from providing supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded
participants for a period of 10 years, effective from 5:30pm on 20 December 2022.
2.
In this statement of reasons, I refer to the banning order made on 20 December 2022 as the
original decision.
3.
Section 99(1), table item 15 of the NDIS Act confirms the original decision can be subject to
internal review (as it is identified as a ‘reviewable decision’).
4.
Section 100(2) of the NDIS Act requires a request for review of a decision to be made within
three months after receiving notice of the decision. I note FFG requested an internal review of
the original decision within the statutory deadline.
5.
Section 100(6A) of the NDIS Act requires an internal reviewer to make a decision either:
a.
confirming the reviewable decision;
b.
varying the reviewable decision; or
c.
setting aside the reviewable decision and substituting a new decision.
Role of the internal reviewer 
6.
In reviewing the original decision, my task was to make the correct or preferable decision in
accordance with the law and any relevant policies of the NDIS Commission. In doing so, I
considered the evidence that was before the original decision-maker, as well as any new
evidence that was before me at the time of making my decision.
7.
In deciding what is the correct or preferable decision, I needed to be satisfied on the balance of
probabilities of any findings of fact that I made.
Decision 
8.
I have decided to vary the original decision.
9.
The variation I have decided to impose is to reduce the duration of the banning order from 10
years to 3 years. In my view, this corresponds with the amount of time it is reasonably expected
for court proceedings that relate to FFG to be finally determined. Upon determination of those
proceedings, the banning order against FFG can be revisited and consideration given to
whether it should be varied any further (or revoked entirely).
1378
T71.1


===== PAGE 209 =====

10. The banning order made on 20 December 2022 (and varied on 8 June 2023), therefore, will
remain in place until 12:00am on 21 December 2025 (AWST), unless it is varied or revoked
earlier.

Relevant legislation 
12. Section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act gives the Commissioner (or her delegate) the power to
‘make an order (a banning order) prohibiting or restricting specified activities by an NDIS
provider, or by a person who was an NDIS provider, if the Commissioner has revoked the
registration of the person as a registered NDIS provider.’
13. Under section 73ZN(3) of the NDIS Act, a banning order may apply generally or be of limited
application, and may be permanent or for a specified period.
14. Section 73ZN(7) of the NDIS Act states that the Commissioner may only make a banning order
against a person after giving the person an opportunity to make submissions to the
Commissioner on the matter.
15. Section 73ZN(8)(b) of the NDIS Act states that section 73ZN(7) does not apply if the
Commissioner’s grounds for making the banning order are, or include, that the Commissioner
has revoked the registration of the person as a registered NDIS provider.
16. Section 73ZN(9) sets out the various requirements surrounding any banning order made as
follows:
'The Commissioner must: 
(a) give the banning order to the person against whom the order is made; and
(b) include in the banning order a statement of reasons for the order; and
(c) if the banning order is against a person who is employed or otherwise engaged by an NDIS
provider or is a member of the key personnel of an NDIS provider—notify the NDIS provider of
the order as soon as possible.’
1380
T71.1


===== PAGE 211 =====

Background and procedural history of matter 
17. From 19 November 2020 to 20 December 2022, FFG was a registered NDIS provider with the
NDIS Commission.
18. On 19 October 2022, the original decision-maker issued FFG with a notice of intention (NOI) to
revoke the registration of FFG and, consequently, make a banning order against FFG. The NOI
invited FFG to make submissions in relation to both the proposed revocation and banning order
by no later than 5pm (AWST) on 16 November 2022 (notwithstanding that no submission
period was required to be afforded to FFG in relation to the proposed banning order, per
section 73ZN(8)(b) of the NDIS Act).
19. On 19 October 2022 the original decision-maker also decided to suspend the registration of FFG
for a period of 30 days until 19 November 2022. The reasons for noting this become evident
below.
20. The evidence supporting the proposed revocation of FFG’s registration, proposed banning
order to FFG and the suspension of FFG’s registration was similar or overlapped considerably.
21. On 31 October 2022, Mr Monks wrote to the original decision-maker to:
a.
confirm he represented FFG; and
b.
seek an extension of 28 days for FFG to provide submissions (presumably in relation to
the proposed banning order and revocation of registration, given the suspension of
registration had already occurred and did not require a submission period to be
afforded to FFG); and
c.
seek a copy of the information held by the NDIS Commission in determining to suspend
the registration of FFG. I note Mr Monks did not make such a request in relation to the
proposed banning order. However, given the evidential similarities noted above, it
appears the original decision-maker adopted a broad interpretation to Mr Monk’s
request and understood him to also be seeking the material that supported the
banning order proposed to be made.
Mr Monks requested the original decision-maker provide a response in relation to these 
requests within 7 days.  
22. The original decision-maker did not provide a response within the requested timeframe.
23. On 15 November 2022, the original decision-maker wrote to Mr Monks granting an extension
of time for FFG to make further submissions in relation to the proposed banning order (as well
as other regulatory action proposed to be taken against FFG and Mrs Reid). The new deadline
for providing submissions was by 5pm (AWST) on 30 November 2022. The original decision-
maker declined to provide the further information sought by Mr Monks on the basis that the
original decision-maker was satisfied the evidence supporting the proposed banning order
against FFG had been adequately disclosed in the notice dated 19 October 2022.
24. On 30 November 2022, Mr Monks provided a single, joint submission that addressed the
regulatory action/s proposed to be taken against both FFG and Mrs Reid (including the banning
order that is the subject of this internal review). I note those submissions alleged the original
1381
T71.1


===== PAGE 212 =====

decision-maker did not respond to Mr Monk’s requests for an extension of time and further 
information. This is not correct. I have been provided with a copy of an email, dated 15 
November 2022, sent at 6:04pm, from WACompliance@ndiscommission.gov.au to 
info@tomandcolegal.com.au, which attached the letter described above at paragraph 23, 
granting an extension of time and declining the request for further information.  
25. On 20 December 2022, the original decision-maker made a banning order against FFG,
prohibiting it from providing supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded
participants for a period of 10 years, under section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act, on the basis that
the registration of FFG as a registered NDIS provider had been revoked. The banning order took
effect from 5:30pm (AWST) on 20 December 2022.
26. On 21 February 2023, Mr Monks, on behalf of FFG, requested an internal review of the decision
to make a banning order against FFG.
27. On 21 February 2023, the NDIS Commission emailed Mr Monks a formal acknowledgement in
relation to the request for an internal review. The acknowledgment invited FFG to make
submissions to be considered as part of the internal review by 5pm on 21 March 2023.
28. On 20 March 2023, Mr Monks provided submissions, on behalf of FFG, to the NDIS Commission.
29. A decision on the internal review was due by 22 May 2023.
30. On 19 May 2023, I wrote to Ms Michelle Frost (Paralegal at Tom & Co Legal Pty Ltd) to inform
her that, in the course of conducting the internal review, I had become aware of further
relevant information (which did not appear to have been considered by the original delegate
when making the decision to issue a banning order against FFG). In order to consider such
information, and afford FFG an opportunity to respond, I sought an extension of 1 month to
finalise the internal review. Further, the extension was sought on the basis that it would also
provide me with an opportunity to carefully and thoroughly consider FFG’s submissions in this
matter given the concerns FFG had raised in relation to the original decision-maker not
properly considering or addressing its submissions.
31. On 24 May 2023, Ms Frost confirmed that FFG did not consent to the proposed extension of
time.
32. On 1 June 2023, I wrote to Mr Monks and provided him with a copy of the further relevant
material that I had considered as part of the internal review, and I informed him that my
preliminary view was to maintain the banning order against FFG. In that letter, I invited FFG to
make submissions to me in relation to both the ‘additional’ material and my proposed decision
to maintain the banning order. I requested FFG notify me, by 5 June 2023, whether it wished to
make submissions or not, following which I would advise the timeframe for making
submissions.
33. On 6 June 2023, Mr Monks verbally advised lawyers for the NDIS Commission that FFG did not
wish to make any further submissions and would await the internal review decision.

T71.1

Findings and Reasons 
34. I have considered the original decision made on 20 December 2022.
35. I am of the view that the original decision was the correct and preferable decision to be made,
however, I disagree with the reasoning for the original decision.
36. Specifically, the aspects of the original decision that I disagree with are the following:
a.
the original decision adopted the same reasons cited for revoking FFG’s registration as
a registered NDIS provider on 20 December 2022. As a separate administrative
decision, there should have been distinct or independent reasons for the banning order
being issued to FFG; and
b.
the original decision was grounded in a broader range of considerations than was
necessary (given the point I have made above at paragraph 36(a)). In my view, the
decision should have been confined to a narrower and more probative set of material,
specifically the evidence that related to FFG’s involvement in perpetrating fraud against
the National Disability Insurance Scheme (NDIS).
37. In summary, my view is that:
a.
by merely reiterating the reasoning for revoking the registration of FFG, the original
decision failed to articulate the specific need or reason(s) for imposing a banning order
upon FFG (in addition to revoking the entity’s registration as a registered NDIS
provider). Although section 73ZN(1)(a) did allow the original decision-maker to impose
a banning order as he had previously ‘revoked the registration of [FFG] as a registered
NDIS provider’, the use of this statutory power is discretionary. The Commissioner (or
her delegates) are not required to ban each NDIS provider whose registration has been
revoked. Rather, a banning order (made under section 73ZN(1)(a)) should, in principle,
only be used if there is some further or compelling reason for such an order to be made
given an NDIS provider will already be constrained from engaging in the NDIS as a result
of the revocation of its registration. In other words, there should be some additional
value, protection or benefit that a banning order provides - that is not afforded by the
revocation of an NDIS provider’s registration – to warrant the imposition of a banning
order.
b.
I acknowledge the original decision-maker had a wide breadth of material before him.
In my view, however, the evidence relating to FFG’s alleged involvement in fraudulent
activity (particularly given the quality and volume of such evidence) should have
constituted the fundamental reason for banning FFG and prohibiting it from having
access to NDIS participants and the broader disability care sector (notwithstanding that
there may be others reasons to support the banning order too).
38. In the paragraphs below, I set out my reasons for reaching this decision.
Consideration of relevant material 
39. In conducting this internal review, I relied upon a circumscribed amount of the material that
was before the original decision-maker, and I also had regard to relevant materials that the
1383
T71.1


===== PAGE 214 =====

original decision-maker did not consider in the context of deciding to issue a banning order to 
FFG.  
40. As a result, I make the following findings:
40.1. - Background Facts
a.
FFG was formerly a registered NDIS provider operating in Western Australia, until 20
December 2022 when a delegate of the NDIS Commissioner revoked the registration of
FFG.
b.
In relation to FFG, Mrs Reid was:
i.
the co-director from 28 August 2020 to 3 January 2021;
ii.
the sole director from 4 January 2021 to 20 October 2022; and
iii.
the co-director from 21 October 2022 to 1 November 2022.
c.
Mr Simon Reid, who is the husband and business partner of Mrs Reid, was a co-director
of FFG from 28 August 2020 to 3 January 2021 and from 1 November 2022 to present.
40.2. – Complaints submitted to the NDIS Commission 
d.
On 10 May 2022, the NDIS Commission received an anonymous complaint (Complaint
#: 4-37380907579) via telephone that alleged FFG had been engaging in fraudulent
activity with respect to NDIS participant, Ms Meghan Goodwin, by claiming against her
NDIS plan for supports or services it had not provided to Ms Goodwin. Specifically, the
complainant alleged that:
“….Megan was a resident at St Barts and took up residency into Forward 
focus group home on the 18/01/2022, Vida had been billing for support 
services dated back from when Megan was still at St Barts, this fraudulent 
activity has been reported to NDIS fraud reporting.” 
e.
On 10 August 2022, the NDIS Commission received a Participant Critical Incident (PCI)
referral from the NDIA, recording a complaint made by Ms Elanor Smith to the NDIA. In
the referral, Ms Smith is identified as an advocate/plan nominee for Ms Goodwin, and
she alleges:
“….financial abuse of PPT’s plan funding, billing for therapy or financial 
counselling while PPT is at TAFE, No support staff at accommodation at all, 
PPT has constantly run out of funding and has had 8 plans since January 
2021. PPT is scared to report to Police until she is able to find alternative 
accommodation due to staff and owner aiming their attention at her for 
reporting issues.” 
f.
On 20 May 2022, the NDIS Commission received a confidential complaint (Complaint #:
4-37833786005) that raised allegations of FFG engaging in fraudulent activity with
respect to NDIS participant, Ms Sarah-Jane Clulow, by claiming against her NDIS plan for
1384
T71.1


===== PAGE 215 =====

supports or services it had not provided to Ms Clulow. Specifically, the complainant(s) 
alleged that: 
“….[Ms Clulow’s] new plan started 2 to 3 months ago. There is already no more

Coordination services funding. [Ms Clulow] will need to go for an emergency 
review but if she is unable to acquire more funding she will be without a support

As the provider runs multiple companies they are double dipping and charging

Uses the excuse that Yaran need more money or they will kick the PWD out.

The above complaints were closed by the NDIS Commission following either:
i.

iii.
the commencement of legal proceedings relating to the alleged fraud.
40.4. – Sworn Statements of Ms Goodwin and Ms Clulow 
h.
On 11 October 2022, Ms Clulow provided a sworn statement to the NDIA, which alleges
(at paragraphs 13-18) that in relation to her NDIS plan:
i.
FFG submitted duplicate invoices for supports and services;
ii.
FFG submitted invoices in relation to supports and services that it had not
provided to Ms Clulow; and
iii.
FFG overcharged or inflated the amount payable for supports and services it
provided.
i.
On 12 October 2022, Ms Goodwin provided a sworn statement to the NDIA. The
statement of Ms Goodwin seems to allude to, or suggest that FFG fraudulently claimed
funds from her NDIS plan (at paragraphs 25 - 26). However, those parts of Ms
Goodwin’s statement are equivocal in their assertion of fraud. As such, I note the
following points made by Ms Goodwin, but attribute minimal weight to them:
i.
Ms Goodwin only received short term accommodation (STA) or respite
supports and services from FFG on two occasions, the first being in early April
2022 for 5 days and the second being from 22 April 2022 to 2 May 2022. Any
other claims submitted by FFG for STA ‘would be false’;
ii.
Any claims made by FFG in relation to self-managed building capacity ‘is false’.
Ms Goodwin has never received such supports or services from FFG; and
1385
T71.1


===== PAGE 216 =====

iii.
‘If there are any claims for house and yard maintenance, there was no yard’ at
the premises in Safety Bay, WA where Ms Goodwin resided nor did FFG provide
any ‘gardening and house maintenance’ at an earlier premises where Ms
Goodwin resided (St Barts, WA) as that facility had ‘their own staff there that
did that’.
40.3. – Criminal charges & legal proceedings 
j.
On 19 October 2022, Mrs Reid was arrested and charged with several offences under
sections 135.1(3) and 400.9(1) of the Criminal Code (Cth), which is contained in the
Schedule to the Criminal Code Act 1995 (Cth). Those charges related to Mrs Reid
allegedly defrauding the NDIS and using FFG as a vehicle through which to perpetrate
that fraud.
k.
Specifically, the charges were that:
i.
between 1 December 2021 and 17 May 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
Australia, Mrs Reid dealt with money or other property, it being reasonable to
suspect that such money or other property was proceeds of crime and at the
time of dealing the value of the money or property was more than $100,000,
contrary to section 400.9(1) of the Criminal Code (Cth);
ii.
between 26 December 2021 and 23 June 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
Australia, Mrs Reid dealt with money or other property, it being reasonable to
suspect that such money or other property was proceeds of crime and at the
time of dealing the value of the money or property was more than $100,000,
contrary to section 400.9(1) of the Criminal Code (Cth);
iii.
between 30 November 2021 and 8 April 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
Australia, Mrs Reid caused payment requests to be submitted, which she knew
to be false, to the NDIA, a Commonwealth entity, with the intention of
dishonestly causing a loss, contrary to section 135.1(3) of the Criminal Code
(Cth); and
iv.
between 18 November 2021 and 19 April 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
Australia, Mrs Reid caused payment requests to be submitted, which she knew
to be false, to the NDIA, a Commonwealth entity, with the intention of
dishonestly causing a loss, contrary to section 135.1(3) of the Criminal Code
(Cth).
l.
In the criminal proceedings relating to these charges, the NDIA lodged a Statement of
Material Facts, dated 19 October 2022, which set out the facts supporting the charges
(the SoFM). The SoFM alleges that, between 26 November 2021 and 22 June 2020, Mrs
Reid and Mr Reid caused 81 false payment requests to be submitted against the NDIS
plans of Ms Goodwin and Ms Clulow, with the intention of dishonestly causing a loss to
the Commonwealth.
m.
In considering the SoFM, I particularly had regard to the following allegations:
1386
T71.1


===== PAGE 217 =====

i.
Mrs Reid and Mr Reid were joint signatories to company bank accounts for FFG,
namely:

ANZ Bank account numbered 016253 152003884 (the FFG ANZ
account); and

Westpac Bank account numbered 036032 736973 (the FFG Westpac
account).
ii.
between 3 February 2021 and 1 September 2022, numerous complaints were
made to the NDIA in relation to the conduct and claiming patterns of FFG.
iii.
on 1 September 2022, the NDIA commenced an investigation into the claims
submitted by FFG.
iv.
The NDIA’s investigation discovered the matters set out below.
v.
on 30 November 2021, the NDIA issued a new plan for NDIS participant, Ms
Meghan Goodwin. On the same day, Mrs Reid contacted the NDIA and
requested a copy of the new plan for Ms Goodwin, which was emailed to her.
Within an hour of making that call, FFG made three separate claims for
payment in relation to supports and services purportedly provide to Ms
Goodwin. The claims were submitted against the plan expiring on 30 November
2021. On 1 December 2021, the NDIA made a payment of $43,004.60 into the
FFG ANZ account. In my view, the claims described in the SoFM appear to be
excessive and falsified. For example, the claims included 398 hours of house
cleaning between 15 October 2021 and 30 November 2021 – this equates to
8.46 hours of cleaning every day for 47 days.
vi.
Zettai Pty Ltd (trading as Assist Plan Managers) (Assist), a registered NDIS
provider that performs the services of a Plan Manager, at various times
received invoices from FFG for supports or services purportedly provided to
various NDIS participants. Assist would then make claims to the NDIA for those
supports or services, receive funds from the NDIA, and then forward those
funds to FFG.
vii.
between 30 December 2021 and 20 April 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate
claims to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to Ms
Goodwin. The claims resulted in the payment of $5979.64 to Assist, who
subsequently transferred those funds to the FFG ANZ Account.
viii.
Ms Goodwin has provided a witness statement that she did not receive the
supports or services purportedly provided in the claims referred to above.
ix.
an NDIA forensic accountant examined (among other things) the statement of
Ms Goodwin, NDIS payments records and banks statements for the FFG ANZ
account. The NDIA forensic accountant calculated that the value of claims
submitted by FFG where no support or service was provided to Ms Goodwin to
be $127,475.30. The SoFM seems to indicate that $78,491.06 of this sum was
1387
T71.1


===== PAGE 218 =====

paid to an associated entity of FFG, namely Hybrid Hive Pty Ltd, and to that 
extent, I have disregarded that proportion of the alleged fraud.  
x.
the NDIA alleges that Mrs Reid, therefore, received money reasonably
suspected of being the proceeds of crime.
xi.
between 24 November 2021 and 22 April 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate
claims to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to Ms
Clulow. The claims resulted in the payment of $86,192.60 to Assist, who
subsequently transferred those funds to the FFG ANZ Account.
xii.
between 25 May 2022 and 22 June 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate claims
to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to Ms Clulow.
The claims resulted in the payment of $33,816.88 to Assist, who subsequently
transferred those funds to the FFG Westpac account.
xiii.
Ms Clulow has provided a witness statement that she did not receive the
supports or services purportedly provided in the claims referred to above.
xiv.
an NDIA forensic accountant examined (among other things) the statement of
Ms Clulow, NDIS payments records and banks statements for the FFG ANZ
account and the FFG Westpac account. The NDIA forensic accountant
calculated that the value of claims submitted by FFG where no support or
service was provided to Ms Clulow to be $120,009.48.
xv.
as a result, the NDIA alleges that Mrs Reid received money reasonably
suspected of being the proceeds of crime to the sum of $120,009.48.
xvi.
Lastly, a former employee of FFG, Ms Leah Norman, who worked for FFG from
14 December 2020 until October 2021, alleges that Mrs Reid encouraged her to
inflate the amount of time she spent coordinating support services for NDIS
participants. Ms Norman alleges that Mrs Reid would “invoice bomb”, meaning
she would claim for the residual funds left in an NDIS participants plan towards
the end of the plan expiry, and Ms Norman fielded complaints from three
separate plan managers about this type of claiming.
Conclusion/s in relation to relevant materials 
41. In light of the above information, I am satisfied, on the balance of probabilities, that FFG
engaged in fraudulent activity, or more specifically, that it was used as a vehicle through which
fraud was perpetrated against the NDIS by its key personnel.
42. I give significant weight to the complaints submitted to the NDIS Commission in relation to Ms
Goodwin and Ms Clulow, the NDIA’s SoFM and the sworn statement of Ms Clulow as these
distinct pieces of evidence all corroborate the same allegation/s of FFG having been involved in
defrauding the NDIS over a substantial period of time. The evidence presents a uniform and
egregious account of FFG’s systems being used, or FFG’s key personnel systemically submitting
false claims for payments against the NDIS plans of NDIS participants.
43. I note FFG has not provided any evidence to negate the fraud alleged above.
1388
T71.1


===== PAGE 219 =====

44. I also acknowledge there are criminal proceedings relating to this matter currently in process
and a verdict has not yet been reached in those proceedings. However, as a national regulator,
the NDIS Commission is not constrained from performing its statutory function to protect and
safeguard NDIS participants whilst there is pending court action. This is particularly true in
circumstances where the NDIS Commission has conducted an independent investigation in
accordance with the legislation governing its operations (i.e. the NDIS Act and NDIS Rules) and
determined that regulatory action is needed immediately to address a serious risk (i.e. financial
abuse of NDIS participants and perpetration of fraud against the NDIS).
45. Another reason why I am not inclined to await the court outcome is that I am satisfied of the
veracity of the evidence before me, particularly given:
a.
the evidence consists of, or involved the forensic examination of FFG’s bank statements
and payments claims submitted by FFG to the NDIA;
b.
the evidence has been gathered and scrutinised by the agency specifically charged with
investigating NDIS fraud (i.e. the NDIA) as well as the Australian Federal Police; and
c.
the allegations against FFG have been made by various individuals and entities who
have consistently described the same pattern of fraudulent claiming by FFG.
46. I acknowledge FFG has made submissions in relation to the evidence in this matter, and I deal
with those below.
The submissions of FFG 
47. I have considered the submissions made by Mr Monks, on 30 November 2022 and 20 March
2023.
48. In summary, the submissions of FFG denied the breadth of allegations put forth by the original
decision-maker, which were that:
a.
FFG was providing support or services to NDIS participants for which it was not
registered (i.e. specialist disability accommodation);
b.
the NDIS Commissions had received over 30 complaints regarding the safety and
quality of supports and services provided by FFG;
c.
the NDIS Commission held information that suggested FFG does not support
individuals to raise complaints they may have directly with FFG, without fear of
retaliation from FFG of its key personnel;
d.
FFG does not have a complaints management and resolution system in place that
ensures complaints are welcomed, acknowledged, respected and well-managed;
e.
FFG does not employ workers who hold the relevant qualifications for their role/s;
f.
FFG had not been acting with respect for individual rights to freedom of expression,
self-determination and decision-making in accordance with applicable laws and
conventions. The written communications of FFG with NDIS participants (or their
family/support persons) included veiled and direct threats, bullying and harassment;
1389
T71.1


===== PAGE 220 =====

g.
FFG was not acting with integrity, honesty and transparency. FFG had knowingly
been submitting false claims for payments to the NDIA, with the intention of
dishonestly causing a loss to a Commonwealth entity; and
h.
FFG had not taken all reasonable steps to prevent and respond to the exploitation
of people with disability. FFG had been seeking to act as both an NDIS provider for,
and the authorised person, on behalf of NDIS participants, to engage with the NDIA
in relation to their support provision/s and funding decisions.
49. I note that FFG provided a significant amount of information (and evidence to varying extents)
to the original decision-maker to support its position.
50. Further, a key aspect of FFG’s submissions was the assertion that the original decision-maker
had failed to afford procedural fairness to FFG, and had not properly considered the
submissions made by FFG when determining to issue the banning order. (Please refer to
paragraphs 53-62 below, where I consider these submissions in further detail).
51. As already mentioned above, I have adopted a narrow(er) approach to this internal review and
based my decision upon a more circumscribed amount of material than that relied upon by the
original decision-maker, that is, I rely specifically upon the fraud that FFG is alleged to have
been involved in. Therefore, in my view, it is not necessary or conducive to go into a detailed
examination of all of the submissions FFG previously made, rather I propose to deal solely with
the aspects of FFG’s submissions that relate to the matters upon which I have formed my
decision (given the remaining matters have fallen away). Before, proceeding to do so, however,
I acknowledge that there were deficiencies and failings by the original decision-maker with
respect to:
a.
sufficiently providing FFG with information about some of the allegations raised
against it (noting that FGG was, on balance, adequately informed of the material
before the original decision-maker); and
b.
clearly communicating his view or analysis of FFG’s submissions. I have reviewed
internal working documents of the NDIS Commission that demonstrate the original
decision-maker did consider the submissions of FFG, however, he was not
persuaded by them. Unfortunately, the original decision failed to clearly articulate
the reason/s for giving minimal weight to FFG’s submissions.
52. The submissions made by Mr Monks that relate to the issue of fraud are found in paragraph 6
and on page 11 of the 30 November 2022 submission, and paragraphs 6-7, 16-17, 19-21 and 23-
24 of the 20 March 2022 submission.
53. In summary, those submissions were that:
a.
FFG emphatically denies the allegation that false claims were submitted for payment to
the NDIA with the intention of dishonestly causing a loss to a Commonwealth entity;
b.
the matter is currently the subject of criminal proceedings and it is inappropriate for
the NDIS Commission to pursue the allegations until the criminal proceedings have
been determined;
1390
T71.1


===== PAGE 221 =====

c.
in the interests of ‘procedural and substantive justice’, FFG’s ‘fitness to hold an NDIS
registration’ should not be decided until the criminal proceedings are determined;
d.
requesting FFG to provide a response in relation to the alleged fraud is inappropriate
and comprises FFG’s legal defence;
e.
the NDIS Commission’s issuance of a banning order to FFG indicates that it has pre-
determined FFG is guilty of the alleged fraud although the relevant evidence has not
been tested in court yet;
f.
FFG is concerned by the linkage of, the banning order made against it, to the criminal
proceedings against Mrs Reid, because in the event that Mrs Reid is found not guilty,
the banning order would remain in place and unjustifiably penalise FFG after the
conclusion of the court matter;
g.
In light of the above paragraph, it would have been prudent for the NDIS Commission
to maintain the suspension of FFG, rather than issue a banning order to it. Adopting this
approach would have protected the interests of all parties, including NDIS participants.
h.
The NDIS Commission has not followed its own Compliance and Enforcement Policy by
issuing the banning order as there were other regulatory actions available to the NDIS
Commission to implement, such as the suspension of FFG’s registration.
i.
The NDIS Commission did not afford FFG procedural fairness (in line with the National
Disability Insurance Scheme (Procedural Fairness) Guidelines 2018) given it failed to
provide FFG with a fair opportunity to respond to all allegations against it.
j.
FFG provided names of witnesses who could support its position in relation to the
allegations against it. The NDIS Commission did not follow up those witnesses.
54. I consider each of these submissions in turn below.
55. In relation to the submission made by FFG at paragraph 53(a) above, I attributed minimal
weight to FFG’s categorical denial of the alleged fraud given FFG has not provided any evidence
to support or exonerate it.
56. In relation to the submissions made by FFG at paragraphs 53(b), (c) and (d) above, I gave
significant weight to these submissions as I acknowledge that the allegations against Mrs Reid
(which implicate FFG) have not been proven to the criminal standard. I also acknowledge and
respect the presumption of innocence in favour of Mrs Reid in relation to the criminal charges.
However, the submissions of FFG ultimately did not dissuade me from my view that a banning
order should be made against FFG. As noted earlier, pending court action does not prevent the
NDIS Commission (or any national regulator) from performing its statutory function/s in the
interim. Rather, it will often be the case that a regulatory body will need to take action for
protective purposes until any associated court process is finalised, particularly in circumstances,
such as the present, where there may be a serious risk involved in the alleged wrongdoing
remaining unaddressed. I consider there would be a significant and indefensible risk associated
with FFG being allowed to continue to engage in the NDIS sector until the criminal proceedings
are determined - the likelihood of further harm to other NDIS participants to whom FFG may
1391
T71.1


===== PAGE 222 =====

provide supports or services (i.e. depletion of their NDIS funding) warrants the NDIS 
Commission imposing a banning order at this time to safeguard NDIS participants. 
57. In relation to the submissions made by FFG at paragraph 53(e), I gave considerable weight to
this assertion. I appreciate that, in the context of criminal proceedings, the standard of proof
that applies is that allegations must be proven beyond a reasonable doubt. I am not purporting
to stand in the shoes of the court or perform its judicial task. The making of a banning order is
an administrative decision, and it only requires that I be satisfied on the balance of probabilities
that FFG has engaged in the conduct alleged, which, I am, given the strength of the evidence
referred to above at paragraphs 41 to 45.
58.
In relation to the submission made by FFG at paragraph 53(f) above, I attributed minimal
weight to FFG’s concerns as it seems the entity does not fully understand the legislative
framework concerning the issuance of a banning order. In the event that the criminal
proceedings determine Mrs Reid is not guilty of the alleged fraud, section 73ZO of the NDIS Act
enables the NDIS Commissioner to vary or revoke the banning order against FFG if satisfied that
it is appropriate to do so. Further, a variation or revocation can be effected on the
Commissioner’s own initiative or upon application by FFG.
59. In relation to the submission made by FFG at paragraphs 53(g) and (h) above, I have given these
submissions minimal weight as they, similarly, indicate that FFG does not fully understand the
legislative provisions regarding the use of suspensions under section 73N of the NDIS Act.
Firstly, if the NDIS commission were to suspend the registration of FFG instead of making a
banning order against FFG, it would not provide a sufficient level of safeguarding to NDIS
participants. A suspension would only prohibit FFG from providing supports and services for
which it must be registered to deliver to NDIS participants. Meaning, FFG could continue to
operate as an unregistered NDIS provider. Secondly, the particular use of the suspension power
that is contended for by FFG is not appropriate. A suspension under section 73N is designed to
be a temporary action (only in effect for 30 days) to address immediate or significant concerns
held in relation to an NDIS provider. It is not intended to be used repeatedly or for a substantial
period of time in relation to the same NDIS provider. If a suspension were to be consecutively
issued, it would signal that a more permanent or enduring form of regulatory action is, in fact,
needed.
60. In relation to the submission made by FFG at paragraph 53(i) above, I gave significant weight to
FFG’s concerns. As noted above, there were some procedural failings or deficiencies associated
with the original decision, which I have given close attention to. To that end, on 1 June 2023, I
notified FFG of the decision I was proposing to making on internal review and provided FFG
with a copy of the material(s) I had relied upon in forming my preliminary view (i.e. the sworn
statements of Ms Goodwin and Ms Clulow, the NDIA SoFM and a summary of the complaints
submitted to the NDIS Commission in relation to Ms Goodwin and Ms Clulow). Further, I invited
FFG to make submissions to me in relation to that information and the proposed internal
review decision.
Most importantly, the matters (or allegations) in relation to which FFG alleges that the original
decision-maker failed to observe procedural fairness, notably, do not form part of my decision.
I have based my decision on distinct considerations – i.e. the alleged fraud against the NDIS –
1392
T71.1


===== PAGE 223 =====

which I have ensured FFG has been sufficiently informed of, and given a fair opportunity to 
respond to.  
61. In relation to the submission made by FFG at paragraph 53(j) above, I have attributed no weight
to the assertion that the NDIS Commission failed to follow up with witnesses whom FFG
identified as being able to support FFG’s position. I can find no evidence of FFG having provided
the original decision-maker (or I) with the names or contact details of relevant witnesses. I note
that, in the context of a separate but related matter, namely the decision to issue a banning
order to Mr Reid, Mr Monk claimed to be in possession of evidence that exculpated Mr Reid of
the wrongdoing he was alleged to have engaged in, but he did not provide such information
(even when requested to do so by the original decision-maker).
Section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act – power to issue a banning order where the registration of a 
registered NDIS provider has been revoked 
62. The banning order issued to FFG was made pursuant to section 73ZN(1)(a) of the NDIS Act.
63. The statutory test for issuing a banning order under this provision is singular. The NDIS
Commissioner (or her delegate) must have revoked the registration of a person as a registered
NDIS provider. Provided that process of revocation had occurred, it was open to the original
decision-maker to impose a banning order.
64. I have been provided with a copy of the decision to revoke the registration of FFG on 20
December 2022. I am, therefore, satisfied that it was open to the original decision-maker to
issue a banning order to FFG.
65. However, as mentioned above (at paragraphs 36-37) the original decision-maker should have
clearly articulated the reason(s) as to why a banning order was warranted in the circumstances.
The mere fact that section 73ZN(1)(a) conferred upon the original decision-maker the power to
use this statutory tool, did not suffice or justify the use of it.
66. Despite the original decision-maker’s omission in this regard, in my view there are compelling
reasons to support or warrant the issuance of a banning order to FFG. Those reasons are that:
a.
FFG was, in effect, used by the key personnel of the company as a vehicle to perpetrate
the alleged fraud against the NDIS. In my view, where an entity has facilitated the
commission of fraud, it is not appropriate for it to remain engaged in the NDIS sector
given the serious risk it poses to NDIS participants (i.e. the depletion of their NDIS
funding);
b.
The revocation of FFG’s registration does not provide a complete or wholesale level of
safeguarding to NDIS participants given that FFG remains capable of providing supports
and services to NDIS participants for which registration is not a requirement. The
classes of supports and services that can be delivered by an unregistered NDIS provider
are vast and would enable FFG to have access to the NDIS plans of thousands of NDIS
participants.
c.
I acknowledge the making of a banning order in respect of FFG will significantly impact
the operations of the business, and I have taken this into account. However, I consider
1393
T71.1


===== PAGE 224 =====

that the competing interest of ensuring NDIS participants, who may not be able to fully 
protect their own interests and who have a statutory right to safe services and 
protection from violence, abuse and exploitation, is paramount and takes precedence 
over the detriment FFG may experience if banned. 
67. Further, I agree with the reasoning expressed by the original decision-maker in paragraphs 27 –
33 of the original decision in so far that he explained the objects of the NDIS Act (contained in
section 3)  as well as section 4(17)(b) of the NDIS Act are factors which also support the
imposition of a banning order against FFG.
68. Therefore, I am satisfied that the making of a banning order against FFG is the correct and
preferable decision in the circumstances.
The NDIS Commission Banning Order Policy 
69. The NDIS Commission Banning Order Policy contemplates that a banning order can be ‘issued
pending the determination of [criminal] charges’ if, in the circumstances, the information and
evidence obtained by the NDIS Commission supports a view that a banning order is warranted
and appropriate.
70. For the reasons already noted above (at paragraphs 41 and 45), I consider a banning order
against FFG to be warranted and appropriate.
Requirement to take proportionate and risk responsive action under NDIS Act 
71. The NDIS Commission is required to conduct compliance and enforcement activities in a risk
responsive and proportionate manner (per section 181D(4)(b) of the NDIS Act).
72. The NDIS Commission’s Banning Order Policy notes that a banning order can have a significant
impact on the recipient and on people with disability, and as such, it will be reserved for
circumstances where it is needed to protect people with disability from the risk of being
exposed to conduct that falls well short of acceptable standards. However, a banning order
should not be for a longer period than is considered necessary.
73. Having regard to the criminal proceedings pending against Mrs Reid (which, as noted above,
implicate FFG in fraudulent activity), I consider that a banning order for a period of 3 years is a
risk responsive and proportionate measure as it corresponds with the amount of time it is
reasonably expected to take for the criminal proceedings to be finally determined by the court.
Upon determination of those proceedings, the banning order against FFG can be revisited and
consideration given to whether it should be varied any further (or revoked entirely). I note a
variation or revocation of the banning order could also take place prior to the determination of
the court proceedings if appropriate or necessary.
74. In the meantime, the banning order will protect NDIS participants from the risk of having
fraudulent claims submitted against their NDIS plans and their funding for disability supports or
services being depleted.
75. The banning order will, therefore, remain in place until 12:00am on 21 December 2025
(AWST).
1394
T71.1

76. The NDIS Provider Register published on the NDIS Commission’s website will be updated to
reflect the banning order made against FFG was affirmed on internal review.

Non-compliance with the revocation
Please note that engaging in conduct that breaches the revocation of registration may be a 
contravention of section 73B of the NDIS Act, insofar as the NDIS rules may require that specified 
classes of supports provided under a participant’s plans be provided only by NDIS providers who are 
registered to provide those classes of supports. A contravention of section 73B attract a civil penalty 
up to $55,500 for individuals.
NDIS Provider Register 
Section 73ZS of the NDIS Act requires the Commissioner to maintain the NDIS Provider Register 
which must include certain information for each person who is a registered NDIS provider. Relevant 
to this matter, section 73ZS(5)(e) permits the NDIS Provider Register to include information about a 
former NDIS provider, including information about the revocation of a person’s registration.

about the revocation will be included on the NDIS Provider Register, available on the Commission’s

16. The NDIS Act s73J requires providers to comply with registration conditions. It is a condition of
registration that providers comply with the Rules. The National Disability Insurance Scheme

(Provider Registration and Practice Standards) Rules 2018 (the Practice Standards Rules) section

17. The Provider is clearly advertising the provision of supports that you are not registered to

Submissions
21. The Commission received a submission on 30 November 2022 (the extended submission date).
22. Your submission notes that a request for further time and further information were not
responded to however, the Commission did respond to this request. This is evidenced by the
Commission’s acceptance of the submission on 30 November 2022 (the extended submission

26. I have carefully considered the information provided in your submissions alongside previous
information outlined in the letter dated 19 October 2022. I note your submission does not
provide the Commission with additional evidence to support your position.
27. As part of my considerations I am obliged to consider the NDIS Act. Section 3 of the NDIS Act
contains the objects of the NDIS Act, which includes:
a.
Supporting the independence and social and economic participation of people with
disability.
b.
Enabling people with disability to exercise choice and control in the pursuit of their
goals and the panning and delivery of their supports.
1401
T72


===== PAGE 232 =====

ATTACHMENT A
NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
7
c.
Protecting and preventing people with disability from experiencing harm arising
from poor quality or unsafe supports or services provided under the National
Disability Insurance Scheme.
28. In giving effect to the objects of the NDIS Act, including those outlined above, section 3(3)(b)
requires regard to be had to the need to ensure financial sustainability of the National Disability
Insurance Scheme (NDIS). Similarly, as set out in NDIS Act section 4(17) it is the intention of the
Parliament that the Commissioner, amongst others, have regard to the need to ensure financial
sustainability of the NDIS in performing functions and exercising powers under the NDIS Act.
29. As a registered NDIS provider, Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd, elected to participate in a
national scheme aimed at assisting those who have permanent and significant disability, and
providing those participants with choice and control over their supports and services, and their
selection of the providers of those supports and services.
30. My view is that the need to protect the interests of people with disability, who may be
particularly vulnerable, and ensure they are kept safe, outweighs the Provider’s interest in
continuing to provide supports or services to people with disability.
31. The matters set out above demonstrates conduct that falls well below the standard and quality
of care expected from NDIS providers when providing supports or services to people with
disability.
32. The information before me in relation to the protracted length of time over which this conduct
took place and continued as well as the instances of similar misconduct against multiple NDIS
participants suggests that Provider’s conduct was systemic in nature.
33. I am also of the view that allowing the Provider to continue providing supports or services to
people with disability would jeopardise public trust in the NDIS.
34. I consider the alleged conduct of the Provider is entirely relevant to my assessment of the
Provider’s suitability to be involved in the provision of supports or services to NDIS participants,
in considering the objects and principles of the NDIS Act.  This is particularly the case as the
parties impacted by the Provider’s actions are NDIS participants.
35. Having weighed all the evidence at hand I reasonably believe that the Provider, has contravened,
is contravening, or is proposing to contravene, the Act.
36. I am therefore satisfied that the power to revoke the registration is available to me and that it is,
in all of the circumstances, a risk responsive and proportionate response.
Philip Halton
